Germrode/H., M.6.5
Dapl. Ing. Gotthold Baats
Hersogin Marienstr.8.
(Reich Patent Office Delegate
in the Japan Committee)
TO: Capt. BRUNNER
Gernrode/M.
Concerning: Patent Contracts with Japan.
Dear Capt. Brunner:
As you told me on 8 June 1945, you wanted to learn in what part of the
German war armament the Japanese had been especially interested, what patent
contracts had been concluded and how much German information had already been
given to Japan.
For the uniform handling of all questions connected with the giving of
information to Japan, the Ministry of Economics in Berlin created the so-called
"Japan Committee". A situation developed which required a liaison office be-
tween the Japanese and the German Industry. German industry did not feel in-
clined to relinquish its newest inventions and blueprints, although very high
payments had been offered, as it considered competition at a later date. It
would rather deliver the equipment wanted by Japan. Owing to personnel, raw-
material and transport difficulties this was impossible, and German industry was
pressed hard to relinquish its inventione and blueprints. German industry re-
quested very large payments to which Japan did not concede.
The Japan Committee took over the settlement of differences. The Jap-
anese wanted to recognize its suggestions and decisions as binding.
The following four men belonged to the Japan Committee:
1. SCHARR,
former Reichsband Director, by order of the Ministry of
Economics, as expert for financial and economic matters.
2. BAATZ,
(the undersigned), Oberregiorungsrat and member of the Ger-
man Patent Office, by order of the armament ministry, as
expert for contract, patent law and technical matters and
for estimates of purchase value of the articles.
3. LENGNER,
Korvettkapitän of the Reserve (civilian occupation: Patent
lawyer) by order of the OKW, as expert for military release
in regard to the equipment requested.
4. LUUK,
for the Reichsgruppe industry, as collection point and ex-
pert for the incoming requests of German industry preparing
patent contracts with Japan.
For the consideration of special questions the Committee could consult:
NIEMETZ, Ministerialrat in the Ministry of Finance.
BEHREND, Geheimrat in the Foreign Office, East Asia Department.
The contracting partners, i.e., the Japanese and the German industrial
firms, prosented their wishes, which sometimes differed, to the Japan Committee
which made a suggestion, almost always accepted by both sides. The contract-
ing party on the German side was alwaye a German firm, on the Japanese side alwaye
the Army or Navy, but never the Japanese Government as such or even a Japanese
firm. The Japanese Army and Navy wanted to have the right to have the patented
equipment produced within the Japanese sphere by a firm chosen by them,
3301

Embassy Secretary SAKAI..
There was no special rapresentative of the Japanese Air Foree in Merlin.
The Japanese were interested in:
Military equipment already tried out;
Military equipment still being tried out;
Military equipment still being planned;
Methods and machine tools for the above mentioned.
Civilian goods which as such had nothing to do with war, but were
used in connection with military equipment, such as radio tubes.
The Japanese paid outright for patents on civilian goods. For patents
on military equipment the German Government repaid the German firms and then
settled those payments according to an agreement with Japan against deliveries
of Japanese goods, particularly raw materials.
Some of the items which were the subject of negotiations with the Jap
anese are listed below. (Unfortunately I have left my lists in Berlin and

For most of this equipment patent contracts were conoluded with sub-
contractors for the manufacture of components protected by patents. The Japanese
needed in particular many manufacturing methods, machine tools, etc., as Jap-
anese production probably was not up to the standard of German produetion.
As I ascertained bofore I left Berlin on 5 April, 1945, Messrs. SCARR

uated in the American sone. If Mr. LUUK is still there, as I assume he is,

and Lasensing Ltbrary.
The main department "Ldcenses and Patents" of the Junkers aireraft and
engine works is divided according to tasks into ths following seetions:
1. Patent Section - Engines
2. Patent Section - Airoraft Construction
Patent Section - Special Fields
Lioense Section
5.
Section for technical and economical reports.
6.
Administration.
The patent sections deal with technical developments of the whole con-
cern in regard to new inventions.' They work in close cooperation with the de-
partments for research, development, engineering and production. These irven-
tions are checked by the patent sections in regard to their novelty, economie
importanee and - under consideration of technical development - in regard to
protection. They are elaborated upon by trained experts as to protection rights
in Germany and, if required in other countries, as to technical, legal, econ-
omic and administrative requirements.
All matters of an administrative nature, such as supervision of renewal
fees, payment of renewal fees, expiration date control, correspondence with anthor-
ities and representatives at home and abroad, and legal matters such as defense
against oppositions, suits for annulments, limitation against third-party rights,
etc., were under the firm's management for the period of time during which the
patent was in force.
Within the activities sketched above, the patent section dealing with
engines concerns itself with internal combustion engines, especially Otto and
Diesel engines including all their accessories, inasmuch as they serve to pro-
pel aireraft. Furthermore, the patent section for engines deals, in matters
of patent law, with propellors, especially automatic variable-pitch propollors
and all those problems connected with these items, such as diving brakes, land-
ing brakes, etc. Another important sphere of activity of the patent seetion
for engines is jet-propelled units for the propelling of aireraft by means of
backward blast and special engines for underwater craft, especially torpedoes.
Finally, this section is concerned with the result of research undertaken in
the field of raw materials, with preference to production, treatment and the
refining of metals used.
The patent section for aircraft construction concerns itself with all
questions regarding patent law as far as matters of production as well as de-
velopment are concerned. This inoludes the various production methods as well
as the extensive number of accessories for the installational aids, armament,
etc.
The patent section for special fields works on all other technical pro-
ductions of the concern, with special emphasis on tools, machine tools and in-
stallations for systematic production. Furthermore, this section deals with
patent matters of firms connected with the concern. This applies particularly
to "Famo" Vehicle and Engine Works, G.m.b.H., Breslau, inasmuch as vehieles,
water brakes and test-stand installations are concerned, as well as for the
patent rights of the machine works at Grafenstaden in regard to machine tools.
The license section is responsible for matters regarding the economie
utilization of those patents and copyrights owmed by the concern. It carries
out the mecessary negotiations and the conclusion of contracts with firms and
authorities at home and abroad.
The section for technical and economic report's serves the screening of
all publications in the technical press, magazines and newspapers at home and
abroad for activities in which the concern is interested. It indexes and files
these publications and brings them to the attention of the offices interested
in the matter.

The Acting Seeretary of State has reeeived a communication
from the Worthern Equipment Company of Erie, Penasylvania setting
forth that they were the owners of patents No. 103,473, No. 124, 807,
No. 117,009, and No. 231,249 granted by the Japanese Government
prior to the war. The communication further states that the
Japanese Government issued permits to manufacture equipment umder
any patent held by a foreign corporation, and inquires whether it
is possible to obtain an accounting of any permits that may have
been issued under the company's patents from December 7, 1941,
through the year 1945.
The Political Adviser is requested to investigate the
matters referred to above and to extend such protection to the
American interests therein as mey be expedient in the ciroum-
stances. It is also requested that the Political Adviser submit
a report of the transactions in question to the Department for
transmission to the Northern Equipment Company.

894.542/6-2846
or indirectly, a number of Japanese patents covering the
manufacture and sale of its boilers in that country. These
boilers were manufactured in Japan by Japanese firms under
license agreements. We have reason to believe that these
license agreements were suspended after Pearl Harbor and
that thereafter the Japanese Navy and others infringed our
patents on a large scale. A similar situation exists in
Manchuria and it is our desire, if possible, to employ an
agent in Japan to investigate such infringement, safeguard
our rights and reinstate or protect our patents and license
agreements to the end that we may eventually resume our
business and regain our markets in Japan and Manchuria.
We understand that a license is necessary in
order to conduct buisness in these places and we would
greatly appreciate any information you can give us as to
how such a license may be obtained for any or all of the
above purposes.
Yours very truly,

In December 1941: at the outbreak of the War, "The
Enemy Property Aiministration Law" was put in force.
It was decided that in accordance with the Law all
shares owned by nationais of enemies of Japan should
be controlled by the administrator, the Yokohama
Specie Bank. The I.G. E. holdings of the T.S.E. shares,
which were on deposit with Mitsui Trust Co., Ltd. were
delivered to the Tokyo Beanch of the Yokohama Specie
Bank.

The increase of capital of the T.S.E. Co. and the
treatment of the shares owned by the I.G.E. Co. at
the time of such increase:
a. Increase of capital in December, 194l.
At the rate of 6 new shares for each 10 old
ahares, 510,574 shares were allotted to the
I.G.E. Co., but as I.G.E. Co. could not remit
the payment on these shares, the Nippon Denko
K.K., one of the subsidiaries of the T.S. E. Co.,
acquired the said shares.
b. Increase of capital 1n February, 1945.
The rate of increase was 1 new share for each
old share. As above mentioned, the shares
held by the I.G.E. Co. were already disposed of
in April, 1943. Therefore, no allotment of new
shares was made to the I.G. E. Co.

Shibaura Electric Company, Ltd. and by other parties in Japan, and
particulars relating thereto, are not ahown in the accompanying
statement since the amount thereof cannot be determined accurately
until fuller information is available to us from Japan.
If any discussion or further explanation is desired by the
State Department or if additional evidence or data are desired at
this time, please let us know. In the meantime, we respectfully
request that the Department take appropriate steps to have our
said interests recognized, protected and safeguarded and reasonable
advance notice given to us before any sale or other disposition is
made of any of the physical or other properties listed in the
accompanying statement and patent lists.

A. JAPAN
I. Patents
(a) Internetional General Electric Company, is entitled to have
assigned to it all patents and patent epplications on inventions
originating with the General Electric Company registered or filed
in the name of Tokyo Shibaura Electric Company or in the name
Pending the execution and delivery of such patent assignments,
International General Electric Company, has or is entitled to
receive licenses, including the right to grant sublicenses, to
make, use and sell, under all such patents of Japan.
International General Electric Company's right to raceive assign-
ments of such patents and patent applications is based on the
provisions of its pre-war patent license agreement with
Tokyo Shibaura Electric Company, dated October 12, 1939, wherein
it is expressly provided that, et the termination of said agree-
ment, said patents and patent applications shall be assigned to
International General Electric Company. Said agreement was termin:
ated or abrogated by World War II, and consequently, International
General Electric Company being entitled, as aforesaid, to receive
such ussignments, is entitled, pending the execution and delivery
thereof, to have and to exercise the licenses and right to grant
sublicenses under such patents, as aforesaid.
(b) International General Electric Company owns 29.63% of the property
rights in each and every patent, patent application and trademark
in the incandescent and fluorescent lamp fields registered in
Japan in the name of Patent-Treuhand Gesellschaft, Osa Industrielle
Beteiligungen A.G., Osram G.m.b.H., or any subsidiary or affiliate
of Osram Konzern of Germany.
International General Electric Company's ownership of the property
rights in said patents, etc. is based on its bona fide purchase
of 21.63% thereof from other partners in the Osram Konzern of
Germany between July 1, 1929 and July 31, 1940. International
General Electric Company owns an edditional 8% interest of the
property rights in said patents, etc. based on its ownership of
24.94% shareholdings in Allgemeine Elektricitaets Gesellschaft,
acquired between July 1, 1929 and December 31, 1940, and on the
ownership by Allgemeine Elektricitaets Gesellschaft of 32.05%
of the property rights in said patents, etc. of the Osram Konzern.

Subject: Japanese Patents
Dear Mr. Martin:
Please advise what is the policy on the use in the United States
of patents which were issued in Japan, but which may not have been issued in
the United States. I understand that those Japanese patents which were
issued in the United States, were appropriated during the war by the Alien
Property Custodian, and application for use may be made to him, but for the
other class of patents, we do not know what policy there is or where to turn
for the information.
Sincerely yours,
H. Wickliffe Rose

My dear Mr. Rose:
Tour letter of September 27, 1946 makes inquiry conceraing
the use in the United States of patents which were issued in
Japan, but which may not have been issued in the United States.
In order to give you a full reply, the Department of State
needs clarification of your question, particularly as regards
present ownership, the location of the owmer, and the exact
status of the patent or invention. If you will be good enough
to furnish these supplemensary details, I shall be pleased to
give you all the information in the possession of the Depart-
ment on this question.
Sincerely yours,
Edwin M. Martin, Chief

My dear Mr. Searlett:

statement in quadruplioate with reference to certain
patent interests of the Northern Equipment Company
in Japan, and an additional sworn statement in quad-
ruplioate with reference to the bank deposits of the

the Politioal Adviser to the Supreme Commander for the
Allied Powers at Tokyo with a request for an appropri-

reply from that officer, I will communicate with you
again.

has recently reported a sample set of the required

officers in Japan have found necessary:

October 18, 1945.
Mr. Benjamin H. Gordon,
 M. Jos. A. Mack,

Subject: Manufacturing License Arrangement
for Kidde Products ia Japan
Dear Mr. Gordon,
It is our understanding that you are going to Japan in order to iavestigate
and eheck up on monies that might be due to American corporations as the result of
agreements in existence with Japanese companies prior to the outbreak of the war o
December 7, 194l. In this connection we would advise that we have had very
satisfactory relations with Mitsui & Company, Ltd. for many years. In the early
years they represented us as a selling agency and in 1933 we entered into a tem
year manufacturing license agreement whereby they were to pay us royalties on equip-
ments of our design which they manufactured and sold. We shall be very happy and
obliged if you investigate and ascertain any facts and make any collection of funds
which should be due us. It is our opinion that we definitely have momey dme. Da
order to give you adequate facts for your investigation, we advise as follows:
1 - On October 9, 1933, we entered into a manufacturing license agreemeat with
Mitsui Bussan Kaisha, Ltd.of Tokio, known in New York as Mistui & Compamy, Ltd.
whereby we granted Mitsui full license rights under several Letters Patent and
Applications in Japan utilizable in fire extinguishing by the use of gas and other
chemicals and for smoke detection through the medium of smoke, theremostats and other
heat actuated devices. The articles actually sold by Mitsui were carbon dioxide gas
extinguishing equipment and smoke detecting systems. The carbon dioxide extinguishing
systems were primarily fitted by Mitsui on boats.
This applies also to the smoke
detector. The carbon dioxide extinguishers were also supplied for industrial installa-
tions involving electrical md oil hazards and for extinguishing fires in aireraft
engines.
2- Kidde agreed and did supply engineering and technical informations a nooded
curing the peace years when the contract was functioning.
3 - The territory covered the Empire of Japan and was extended to cover Manchoukuo.
4 - The term of the contract was for 10 years and was for an exclusive lioense.
The contract could be terminated at any time by certain notices. No notice of
termination wss at any time received. No indication was set forth in the contract
as to what was to be the relationship between our company and Mitsui upon the
termination of the agreement. It was of course presumed on the part of both
parties that business relations would be continued on a rimilar basis which would
be mutually beneficial after October 8, 1943.

=== Page 171 === (confidence 0.99)
ter Kidde & Company, Inc.
SHEET 2 DATE
10/18/45 To .
5 - Mitsui were to pay us a royalty of l0% on the consumer's price for all equi
sold and delivered, with the exception that the royalty was to be 5% when the
ment was used on Mitsui's own vessels or for Mitsui's own account. Royalty stat
ments were submitted in good faith by Mitsui. Payments of royalty were paid up
through the 6 month period ending October 31, 1940. Since that time no further
payments have been made. As a matter of fact the payment covering the period endi
1940 was made in the spring of 194l. Since that time there was practically no
correspondence between the two companies. It is quite evident that many boats
were constructed and delivered from November 1, 1940 through 0ctober 8, 1943 and our
company is justly entitled under the agreement to a royalty as above outlined.
It is to be noted that ships could have been contracted for prior to the last
royalty payment cate of October l940 which would have equipments ordered and fitted
in later years which means therefore that many ships contracted for in the early
part of 1940 are definitely to be accounted for with royalty reports and payments
which have never been received. Based on payments made, we feel that we should
collect a minimum of $7500. to $10,000. per annum in royalty.
6 - Mitsui Bussan Kaisha was the contractual party to the agreement signed. Mitsui,
however, had an arrangement with one of their subsidiaries, Tokio Keiki Seisakusho,
which manufactured and marketed the equipment of our design covered by the license
agreement.
Judging by the facts that it was a customery procedure for Japanese ship-
owners to fit what we call our Lux-Rich system as manufactured by the Japanese
company and which is the carbon dioxide gas extinguishing system combined with
the smoke detecting system and it is pretty well concluded that they built a very
large number of freighters in the period from early 1940 during the duration
of this contract and even later that substantial monies are rightfully and justly
due our company. It is our opinion further that we have a right to conclude that
if such equipments were manufactured, sold and installed on vessels or other
installations since the termination of the agreement in October 1943, that royalties
are likewise due; because there has been no termination of the arrangements.
We shall be pleased to supply any additional data that you might require
and are prepared to submit documents to substantiate the statements made above at an
time.
We shall likewise be glad to cooperate in any way which you may suggest and
trust that you will be able to put our problem before the allied authorities so
that a proper claim can be entered.
Again thanking you very kindly for any effort you may put into investigation
of this problem, we are
Very truly yours,
WALTER KIDDCOMPANY, Inc.
A.M./poxseyVice-President.

DISCULSION
1. Diseuseion of the problem and the recommendatiems set ferth im
this paper is, in the main, contained in Ammex to Anpandix "C" prepared

2. It is proposed in paragraphs 9 and 10, Appendix "C", that sCAr
be authorised, as an interim measure, to invoke compulsery lieansing against
existing and future Japancss patents on a case by case basis fer reasoms

propocals in the ECLFP report D-59/46 (referred to at paragraph 5, Ammmmiix
"A") which has, with the approval of the President, become the statement of
the positiem to be taken by United States par icipants in the Allied Comtrol
Authority's formulation and application of a quadripartite policy fer the
treatment of German domestio and foreign patents. That statement prepeses
cempalsery licensing of all existing and future patents in Germany at
reasomable royalties to allepplicants.
3. This paper does not propose a parallel recommendation with regard
to Japameso patents becauso it is censidered adviaable to postpone pre-
sentation of such a position for comsideration by FFC until its status as
policy of the Allied Control utherity for Germany has reached a more
definite stage.

Dear Mr. Martin,

13-2947
894.542/4-147
Thank you for your letter of 29 March.
Miss Scrogin requested that we return the Japanese
pens, one of which I thought too closely resembled
our Vacumatic. I think if you will refer to the
returned pen you will readily agree with me. Of
course, the Japanese product is very inferior, but
obviously it has been copied from our Vacumatic
design.
Unfortunately, the patents we as well as other
manufacturers were able to obtain in Imperial
Japan were practically valueless, as the Japanese
Patent Office in the opinion of most manufacturers
purposely limited the protection granted to foreigners
to the point where the inventions could be readily
copied. Of course, we did not have access to the
Courts on the grounds of unfair competition, but I
know that no successful action was ever brought
in Japan on the grounds of unfair competition even
in instances where the Japanese products were
practically duplicates of the American made products.
5
Therefore, our Japanese patents will probably be of
no important value to you.
I think one of the very important things we can do
during the occupation period in Japan is to collaborate
with the Japanese Patent Office and attempt to influence
C
them as to the advisability of giving proper patent
S
protection to foreigners. Proper protection in Japan
would help lift export and import barriers existent
R
prior to the war.
C

=== Page 217 === (confidence 0.98)
The Parker Pen Company
1 April 1947
Page 2 -- Mr. Edwin M. Martin
Japanese imitations of Parker and other fountain
pens were sold all over the world, and in many
instances the American product was so closely copied
that many evidences of confusion resulted. I have a
complete record of the many Japanese infringements
of our products, and if of interest to the Department
I shall be very glad to bring it down to Washington
some time and go over it with you.
In the interim, you might call Miss Scrogin and
ask her to send over the sample Japanese pens returned
to her, one of which I thought was too close to our
Parker Vacumatic.
Again, thank you for your interest in the matter.
Very truly yours,
The Parker Pen Company

My dear Mr. Middleton:
Your letter of June 11, 1947 to the Secretary of War concerning
Japanese patents, has been referred to the Department of State for
reply.
894.542/6-1947
The subject of foreign-owmed patents in Japan is now under
disaussion by the Department and other interested agencies of this
Government, and it is hoped that a policy governing the treatment
of patents owned in apan by United States citizens and by nationals
of the other United Nations will be announced in the near future.
Among other provisions, this policy will probably provide for the
return to their owners of patents held in Japan before the war by
United States citizens.
With regard to your statement that there is a concern in Japan
offering apparatus for sale which comes under patents owned by your
client, the Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers has taken
measures to prevent the export of goods from Japan if such exports
constitute an infringement of United States patents, or other United
Nations patents. The following statement is quoted from a directive
Jec
to the Japanese Government dated May 20, 1947, and refers to the
preparation of goods for export:
"This merchandise offered for export does not
in any way constitute an infringement of
American or Allied Nations owned trademarks,
copyrights or patents."
C
.S
This certification is required to be indorsed by an officially
A1
authorized agency of the Imperial Japanese Government, and must
appear on all forms prepared in connection with merchandise for
export.
Pending
Mr. Arthur Middleton,

BEPARTMENT OF STATE
Dear Mr. Vernon:
Subject: Japanese Patent No, 172865
In the first quarter of 1946 I represented the State
Department in Japan on the Textile Mission to survey the rayon in-
dustry. In addition to our mission report, which was filed with
General MacArthur and which the State Department subsequently pub-
lished, I wrote and published through the Textile Research Institute
a book on the rayon industry of Japan which includes the background
material I thought would be of general interest.
In Japan I found a development of a certain kind of
rayon staple fiber which is described at length in my book. I have
since learned that Japanese Patent No. 172865 was issued after the
end of the war. I cannot obtain a copy of it, therefore, from among
those that were impounded because they do not have it. I am told by
the Textile Division at SCAP that they cannot send the copies of any
patents from Tokyo pending the establishment of policy in Washington.
I have covered the whole subject with Mr. Green of the Department of.
Commerce who had to do with such technical information, and with the
Central Intelligence Group of the Army which has the impounded patents
894.542/
and is processing them. I then talked with Colonel Hartmann, Civil
Affairs Division of the Army who refers me to you as the one who can
issue instructions to Tokyo to let me have a copy of the patent in
S
question.
I would like specifically for such instructions to
cen
be issued as we are interested in seeing that particular patent.
At the same time it will be interesting and valuable to have a
7247
clarification of policy concerning patents issued in Japan so that
we can get on with commercial relations with that country which are
now about to be opened up. I believe clarification of policy on
this subject will be most helpful in that direction.
Since a patent is a public disclosure, we can think
of no reason why the one referred to above should be held back since

this country.
Lieutenant Colonel Harold S. Tate, Chief of the

FOR THE POLITICAL ADVISER.
Reference letter AGO, Washington, D. C., AGAO-C O72
(15 Nov. '46) WDSCA regarding infringement of patents of
Parker Pen Company and lst Ind. by GHQ, Supreme Commander
for the Allied Powers, APO 500, 13 January 1947.
The following letter has been received from the Parker
Pen Company of Janesville, Wisconsin. It is forwarded for
your information and such action as you consider appropriate.
QUOTE. Dear Mr. Martin: Thank you for your letter of
29 March.
Mrs. Scrogin requested that we return the Japanese pens, one
of which I thought too closely resembled our Vacumatic. I
think if you will refer to the returned pen you will readily
agree with me. Of course, the Japanese product is very
inferior, but obviously it has been copied from our Vacumatic
CS/A
design.
Unfortunately, the patents we as well as other manufacturers
were able to obtain in Imperial Japan were practically value-
less, as the Japanese Patent Office in the opinion of most
manufacturers purposely limited the protection granted to
894.5421
foreigners to the point where the inventions could be readily
copied. Of course, we did not have access to the Courts on the
grounds of unfair competition, but I know that no sucdessful
action was ever brought in Japan on the grounds of unfair com-
petition even in instances where the Japanese products were
practically duplicates of the American made products. There-
fore, our Japanese patents will probably be of no important
7-23
value to you.
I think
2

=== Page 239 === (confidence 0.97)
AIRGRAM SENT
CONFIDENTIAL
Department of State
UNCLASS IFTED
A-74
NO.
Washington,
July 23, 1947
TIME
-2-
2221
I think one of the very important things we cen do during the
occupation period in Japan is to collaborate with the Japanese
Patent Office and attempt to influence them as to the advisability
of giving proper patent protection to foreigners. Proper pro-
tection in Japan would help lift export and import barriers
existent prior to the war.
Japanese imitations of Parker and other fountain pens were sold
all over the world, and in many instances the American product
was so closely copied that many evidences of confusion resulted.
I have a complete record of the many Japanese infringements of
our products, and if of interest to the Department I shall be
very glad to bring it down to Washington some time and go over
it witk you.
In the interim, you might call Mrs. Scrogin,(Commerce Ext. 2282)
and ask her to send over the sample Japanese pens returned to
her, one of which I thought was too close to our Parker Vacumatic.
Again, thank you for your interest in the matter.
Very truly yours,
The Parker Pen Company

nationals of the United Nations in Japan, and the resumption of
normal funotioning by the Japanese Patent Office. The proposals
of this Government will then be considered by the Far Eastern Com-
mission.
Regarding your inquiry concerning the designation of an agent
to recover your patent rights and royalties, you may, under present
procsdures, acting through an agent holding a power of attormey
rT
granted by you, apply for the release of the royalties which have
been deposited to your credit in Japan. The use of the funds will,
however, remain subject to theater regulations. With regard to your
patent property, it would be impracticable at this time to attempt
to recover such rights through an agent. Your interests in the
patents is protected by directives of General Headquarters of the
Supreme Commander to the Japanese Government. At the present tims,
arrangements are being made to have the Japanese Government provide
C
all of the detailed information regarding the status of the patent
S
properties owned by persons of other than Japanese nationality at
A1
the outbreak of the war. This work is preliminary to the restora-
tion of these rights to their former owners.
Appropriate
Mr. R. H. Wood,
Goneral Coumsel,
Westinghouse Air Brake Company,
Wilmerding, Pennsylvania.

=== Page 246 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure No. 1 to Despatch No. 1280, dated September 15, 1947, from
the United States Political Adviser for Japan, Takyo, on the sub ject
"Infringement by Japanese of Patents of Parker Pen"Company".
COPY
TIE PARKER PEN COPANY
Janesville, Wisconsin, U.S.A.
22 July 1947
Patent Property Division
Civil Property Custodian
General Headquarters
Supreme Commander for the Allied Povers
Japan
Dear Sirs,
We have had submitted to us by the General Products Division, Commo-
dities Service, Department of Commerce, two Japanese made fountain
pens which are cheap simulations of our Vacumatic pen which, although
we haven't any control in Japan, we feel should be prevented.
I have neither samples nor photographs of the two pens that were sent
to us by the Department of Commerce. However, Mrs. Sara Y. Scrogin,
General Products Division, Commodities Service, Department of Commerce,
Washington, D. C. can furnish you with either the samples she has or
photographs thereof.
There isn't any question but what a good standard fountain pen can be
made in Japan that does not imitate the products of American manu-
facturers.
Prior to the war, due to the fact no American manufacturer could obtain
anywhere near adequate patent protection on his products in Japan, we
were faced with cheap Japanese imitations of our products in practically
every export market, and this Company spent thousands of dollars bring-
ing actions against the importers in countries like Argentina, Brazil,
et cetera, wherein the Japanese imitations appeared. We tried vainly to
stop the manufacturers of the imitations at the source, namely, in Japan,
but the Japanese Patent Office and courts turned deaf ears to all of our
complaints.
It would appear that while we are still active in Japan from a military
standpoint, much can be done to clear up the situation with which the
foreign manufacturer is faced as respects Japanese infringements.
within the last two weeks we have received a Japanese made imitation
of our "5l. pen which we have very carefully covered by patents in
practically all of the major countries of the world, but which we were
unable to patent in Japan due to the antipathy the Japanese Patent
Office and courts carry out as against American applicants for patents
in Japan. Unfortunately, I have only one sample of the infringing "51"
pen, and therefore I am enclosing herewith a photograph of the same.
The manufacturer of this product is Nakata Co., Ltd., Japan.
We

=== Page 247 === (confidence 1.00)
Encl. No. 1 to
Tokyo's 1280,
September 15, 1947
-2-
We wish to make formal complaint to the Japanese Government against
the production by its citizens of fountain pens simulating those of
Parker manufacture.
Very truly yours,
The Parker Pen Company
/2/ Ivan D. Tefft
Ivan D. Tefft
k/s

=== Page 248 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure No. 2 to Despatch No. 1280, dated September 15, 1947, from
the United States Political Adviser for Japan, Tokyo, on the subject
"Infringement by Japanese of Patents of Parker Pen Company"
COPY
GENERAL HEADQUARTERS
SUPREE COLLMANDER FOR THE ALLIED POWERS
APO 500
Tolyo, 6 September 1947
AG O72 (6 Sep 47)CPC/PP
The Parker Pen Company
Janesville, Wisconsin
Attention: Mr. Ivan D. Tefft, Patent Counsel
Dear Sir:
Reference is made to your letter of 22 July 1947 to General
Headquarters, Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers, concerning
Japanese infringements of The Parker Pen Company patents.
The Japanese Government has been specifically required by
directives to preserve and protect patents, utility models, designs,
trademarks and copyrights owned by nationals of the United Nations
on 7 December 1941.
The Japanese Government has reported that it has issued an offi-
cial notification to interested concerns that the infringement of
United Nations industrial property is liable to strict punishment
and that suspected cases of infringement should be reported.
The Ministry of Justice and the Bureau of Patents and Standards
of the Japanese Government have coordinated their operations to ensure
adequate protection of patents and trademarks belonging to United
Nations nationals. The Ministry of Justice is now investigating
specific cases of infringement.
Attention is invited to the fact that protection afforded by exist-
ing directives extends only to that property which existed in Japan on
7 December 1941. Because of legal, administrative and enforcement
difficulties involved, no action can be taken at the present time to
protect patent and trademark property which has never had legal exist-
ence in Japan. However, the protection of all patent and trademark
property of The Parker Pen Company which existed in Japan on 7 December
194l is receiving the full attention of General Headquarters, Supreme
Commander for the Allied Povers and the Japanese Government.
Sincerely yours,
/s/ R. M. IEVY
Adjutant General

SECRET
V. Resune of SWNCC 369, Treatment of
Japanese Domestic and Foreign Patents and Utility Models
1. The Japunese Patent Office shall continue to operate, subject
to the following:
a. All United Natlons-owred patents existing as of 7 December
194l or grunted thereufter on pendin, applications, shall be restored,
upon reqlest, without payment of fees that have accrued during the in-
terim, and siall be extended for a period equal to that from 7 December
l94l to date of restoration. If rcyalties have accrued in interim the
owner may elecs to receive the royalties in lieu of the extension.
b. No existing putents, or rights restored or acquired during
period c occupation, or presumptive ri hts to eventual issuance of
United Nations-owned Japanese patents under provisions for priority of
filing, hall be permi tted to interfere with recovery of Japanese econ-
omy. Compulsory licensing, under patent laws, with reasonable monetary
compensation, vill be invoked.
c. The United Nations owners of patents in their own countries,
who were prevented from filing applications for corresponding patents
in Japan by reason of the war, shall be entitled to priority of filing.

VI.
SUMMARY OF GE-MAN PATENT ACCORD (LONDON ACCORD)
DATED 27 JULY 1946, SIGNED BY TWELVE NATIONS
1. The general effect of the Accord is to make available
to the nationals of all participating governments full rights
to use, without payments of any royalties, all former wholly
German-owned patents issued by those governments subject to the
protection of existing rights lawfully acquired prior to 1 Aug-
ust l1946 by non-Germans. In addition, where a participating
government makes availuble to its own nutionels rights under
other patents where there was formerly a Gernun interest, it
must grunt equal rights to nationals of other purticiptting
governments. Provision is made for the interchange of infor-
mation through a central office to be maintuined by the French
Government. The number of patents in the varlous countries
which will be affoted y the Accord, although not definitely
ascertuined, 1s klown to be well above l00,Oc0.
2. Dispositlon in this manner will remcve the possibility
that these patents can ecome a barrier to irternational or
domestic trude.

5. Copyrights.
The legislution of Jupan on copyrieht--three Imperial
Ordinunces of 1887 and the 1aw of 1894--wus replaced, prepatory
to the accession to the International Copyriht Union, by the
Act on Copyr1 ht of Murch 3, 1899. This Act was amended by the
Act of June 14, 19l0, following the revision of the International
Convention at Berlin in 1908, and further by the Act of Auust 19,
1920. Uther amendments were udded in 1931, 1934 and 1941. The
Act of l9lo, in particuler, added "architectural works" to be pro-
tected; it amended the provision concerning reproduction of news.
paper articles in accorlunce with the Berlin Convention; 1t abolished
the necessity of rogistrution of copyricht; it provided for the
protection of all trans ution, not only of luwful ones. The act
of 1920 added musical and dramatico-musical works to the 1ist of
19

=== Page 288 === (confidence 0.98)
works proteoted by the law, although this, in its broad terms, al-
ready included the same.
vorks Protected. The law (Art 1) enumerates writines,
lectures. puintings, desins, sculptures, plastic works, and photo
raphs, und further includes all works in the domain of literature,
sciences and the urts. The luw of 18y9 contained an explicit exception
with rogard to works of architecture (art. 52.) This exception
was ubol1sned by the umending act of l9l0. Furthermore, the Act
of 1920 udded musical and dramatico-musical works. The protection
of photogrunhs extends to "works obtuined by a process anulogous
to the photouranhic art" (Art. 26).
Oral works are included in the protection of the law, with
the exception of speeches and addresses mude before Courts and in
deliberative assemblies and political meetings (art. 11).
Of derivative creations, the law protects compilation
(art. l4), reproductions of works of art by an art different from
that of the originul, provided they are made lawfully (Art. 22).
Translutions are always protected against infringement as original
works (Art. 21).
The law in several articles speaks of works "publishod,
presented or executed", which indicates that the meaning of "pub-
licution" for dramutic or musical works is different from that of
literary works in general (cf. article 35, third paragraph).
Persons Protected. The law proteots all national authors
and thoir heirs and assi ns. No requirement is made that their
works snould be published for the first time in Jupun. Foreigners
are protectod as lationals, provided there are no special stipu-
lations in troaties or conventions, but in the absence of such
2
stipulatfons, forei ners are protected only if their works have
been published for the first time in Japanese territory (Art. 28).
Public uuthorities, educational establishments, temples,
associations, societies and any other corporations whatsoever,
decluring their authorship of works published, presented or executea
by them, enjoy copyright (art. 6). The law also contains provi-
sions for joint authors (Art. 13).
Fornalities. Article 15 of the law of 1899 provided
for registrution of the copyright. This was not a cond tion to
1ts acquisition. It was roquired prior to the instituting of a
civil action for infringement of a work published, executed or
presented (art. 15, pars. 1 and 2). The amending Act of 1910
abolished this provision of Article 15. The remaining puragraphs

=== Page 289 === (confidence 0.96)
of Article 15 are still in effect. They provide for recording
of ussignments, transfers, etc., and also for the right of authors
of anonymous or pseudonymous works to register their true names.
The person appeuring as author of the worx published
shall be presumed to be such until the contrary is proved. For
anonynous and pseudonymous works, the editor uopearing on the book
stands in the author's stead. In case of dramatic or musical works,
which are not yet "edited," the autlhor is deemed to be the one an-
nounced in the prolram, and, in his absence, the producer.

In reply refer to

My dear Mr. Dickinson:
894.542/4-2648
I refer to your letter of April 26, 1948 in which
you request information concerning the status of your
prewar Japanese and Manchurian patents and patent appli-
cations, and what can be done toward recovering them.
ρ
With respect to the status of your patents and
patent applications in Japan, you are undoubtedly correct
in stating that they were seized by the Japanese Govern-
ment under wartime legislation. A majority of American-
owned patents were so treated, although a sizeable number
lapsed for non-payment of renewal fees. Plans are now
being made to permit foreign nationals to apply for re-
storation and revalidationdf their prewar patents and
applications for patent which were sequestered or which
lapsed. A public announcement to this effect is anticipa-
ted, probably before the end of the year.
We have no information as to the current status of
American-owned patents in Manchuria and can, unfortunately,
hold out little hope at the present for the recovery of
such rights.
Sincerely yours,

=== Page 301 === (confidence 0.94)
ile
SIGNED & RECEIVED
SECRET
IN SANACC
MEMORANDUM POR THE SECRETARY, SANACC
SUBJECT: SANACC 369/1, Treatment of Jananasa Domeatia and
Foreign Patents.
It is requested that prior to distribution of this
paper for final SANACC approval, paragraph 2 of Appendix "C»
be amended to read:
2.The term "patent", when used herein in
relation to the Japanese patent system, shall be
interpreted to include those patents designated
as "registered utility models" and "registered
designs" under Japanese law, but shall not include
trade-marks and copyrights.
894.542/4-3048
This minor change in the coverage of the paper is
based upon more complete information received since it was
originally drafted. At that time the term "design" was
interpreted to be a type of property right akin to a
copyright. It has now been determined, however, that
"designs" as protected by Japanese law are so-called
"industrial designs", closely similar to patents and,
accordingly, should logically be covered by a policy
paper on patents.
Charles E. Saltzman
State Department Member

=== Page 303 === (confidence 0.98)
DRAPT
REPORT ON MEETING BETWEEN REPRESENTATIVES OF NFTC
JAPAN PATENT SUB-COMMITTEE AND REPRESENTATIVES OF
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
Hew State Department Building--Washington, D. C.
10:00 a.m., Wednesday, April 21, 1948
Present: Philader P. Claxton - Department of State
Roswell H. Whitman
Roger Dixon
n
R. Barr Smith
n
W. E. F. Bradley
- Otis Elevater Company
J. A. Holton
- Underwood Corporation
A. T. Brown
- International General Electric Company
Joseph B. Brady
- National Foreign Trade Council, Inc.
During the conversation, the representatives of the Department
of State indicated the following concerning the treatment of patents in
Japan:
1. Japanese Patent Office.
Some records have been destroyed but the office is
operating to a limited extent. It was understood that
no applications are being accepted at the present time.
2. Present status of Japanese Patent Law.
The pre-war law is still in effect.
3. Proposed changes in law.
Only minor changes in present law are contemplated.
The proposed modifications will affect:
a. Provisiens concerning secret patents.
b. The amount of fees.
There is no intent to modify Japanese Patent Law in any
of its basic features.

=== Page 304 === (confidence 0.99)
DRAFT
-2
4. Application for patents.
It is expected that applicetions for patents will be
accepted from foreigners and Japanese before the end of 1948.
5. Patents owned by allied nationals.
a. Beneficial owmership will be recognised.
b. An extension of time equivalent to that lost because
of the war will be granted to owners. Generally
the provisions will be the same as those incorporated
in the treaties of peace with Italy and Balkan countries.
(See text of Annex IV, Italian Peace Treaty, copy
attached.)
c. Provision will be made for payment of back taxes and
reinstatment and of patents cancelled by Japanese
Government under war legislation.
d. No policy is contemplated which would automatically
mitigate against licensing agreements between allied
and Japanese nationals or the exercise of patent
rights through Japanese firms.
. No guarantee concerning royalties earned by foreign
nationals during the war will be made by SCAP.
6. Comments by NFTC's representatives.
NFTC's representatives invited the attention of
Department of State's representatives to the program which had
been adopted by the Committee. (See Ref. No. M-589) In
accordance with this program, it was stated that although the
United States Patent Law was preferable to the Japanese law,
the Japanese law was considered to be quite workable. However,
attention was invited to several features of the Japanese law,
modification of which might be considered some time in the future.

=== Page 305 === (confidence 1.00)
DRAFT
-3
They were as follows:
a. The unusually severe remedies provided by the law.
h. The provisions for opposition proceedings which tended
to encourage litigation.
c. Provisions requiring the annual payment of taxes which
frequently imposed hardships on inventors of limited
resources.
d. With reference to the working provisions, it was noted
that there was a general trend away from such requirements;
and it was expected that the International Convention
to be held this June in Lisbon might recommend deletion
of all such requirements.
7. Trademarks.
The same general provisions contemplated with reference to
patents will be followed. To date, there has not been as much
progress in the field of trademarks as there has been in the
field of patents.
8. Recommendation of State Department.
It was suggested by the State Department's representatives
that members of the Council report the extent and type of
their patents in Japan, and submit recommendation as to specific
treatment which should be followed. Such information and
recommendations concerning patents would be forwarded to the
reparations and restitution unit of SCAP for implementation.

to the meeting that the Japanese Patent Office is probably now
receiving applications for patents from Japanese nationals. Your
statement concerning foreign nationals is correct.

which lapsed for non-payment of fees.

STANDARD FORM NO. 64
Fies
TO
2
Mr. Saltzman
DATE:
May 18, 1948
FROM :
SUBJECT:
Brief - SANAC 369/1, Treatment of Japanese Domestic
and Foreign Patents.
The attached paper includes as its Appendix a note for presentation to
FEC which contains the proposed policy of the United States Government on the
above subject. The policy provides for the following:
a. The restoration and revalidation, upon request of the owner, of
all patents existing in Japan on December 7, 1941, or granted thereafter on
applications filed before that date, which were then owned or applied for by
foreign nationals and which have since lapsed or have been invalidated or
sequestered without the owners' consent. Restoration is to be made within a
reasonable period and in a manner as may be provided by SCAP.
b. The extension, at the request of the foreign owmer, in a manner
prescribed by SCAP of the period of duration of any Japanese patent owmed or
applied for as in a. above beyond its normal expiration date for a period equal
to that from December 7, 1941 to a suitable date fixed by SCAP if the owmer
c. The payment of royalties to the foreign nationals for any use
made of their patents prior to the date of restoration.
d. Reinstetement, upon request of foreign nationals, of applicationa
pending on December 7, 1941.
8
X
The paper also provides for applications by nationals of countries at war
with Japan for patent rights in Japan on their inventions. It also continues
in operation the Japanese Patent Office on condition that no claims of secret
patents be maintained.
The paper further provides that no existing right on patents in Japan should
be permitted to interfere with such production, use, sale or importation as
SCAP may deem desirable.
Japanese owned patents in foreign countries should be treated as follows:
a. All governments should be urged by FEC to terminate all vestige of
Japanese ownership or interest in existing patents and applications.
b. On inventions made by Japan subsequent to December 31, 1945, SCAP
may permit Japanese nationals to file application where he determines that such
action will insure access to foreign markets or provide foreign exchange, pro-
vided that the Japanese nationals agree to assign foreign patents to SCAp or to
the Japanese Government, and provided that the filing is subject to the consent
of the countries concerned.
0.817

=== Page 317 === (confidence 0.98)
- 2 -
c. With regard to inventions referred to in b. above which are not
selected to be filed for patent in foreign countries, SCAP may authorize the
applications or descriptions to be published in those foreign countries where
such action will prevent the granting of patents to other parties.
This paper has received the proper clearance within the State Department
on conditions that one minor change of wording is made, as noted in the attached
pemprandum, and there are no objections from a military point of view from JCS.
The Army participated in drafting the paper, SCAP's views are incorporated
and it is not anticipated that any points will be raised by the Army.
I recommend that you approve the subject paper.
0-817

My dear Mr. Martin:
I refer to your letter of June 10, 1948 in which
you inquire whether it is possible for an American
citizen to deal with a citizen of Japan for the manu-
facture of an item covered by United States patents.
All arrangements of the type to which you refer are
subject to licensing by the occupation authorities in
Japan. It is suggested, therefore, that you make applica-
ment. Such application should be directed to the Supreme
Commander for the Allied Powers, APO 500, c/o Postmaster,
San Francisco, California.
There are, however, several observations we can make
which may be helpful to you in determining your future
course of action. First, one of the major factors deter-
mining the acceptability to SCAP of a proposed business
arrangement between a Japanese and a foreign national is
its probable effect on Japan's foreign exchange position.
For example, an arrangement which would result over a
C
period of time in a net payment to a foreign national
S
would not be likely to receive approval, whereas an arrange-
V
ment which resulted in a net payment to Japan would, if
otherwise acceptable, be approved.
Secondly,

MEMORANDUM FOR: JAPANESE GOVERNMENT
SUBJECT:
Licensing of Government-Owned Patents
1. Reference is made to memorandum for the Japanese Government,
file AG 072.(8 Mar 46)ESS/IE, SCAPIN 802, 8 March 1946, sub jeot, "Patent
Rights and Restrictions Concerning Patent Rights."
2. The Japanese Government or its instrumentalities are hereby
authorized to grant licenses on patents or utility models held in the
name of the Japanese Government or any of its ministries, sub-divieions
or agencies, except those patents or utility models which, since
1 January l930, have at any time been declared to be seoret. Such.
licenses will be granted only on a non-exclusive, non-disoriminatory
basis, and for reasonable royalties.
3. a. The Japanese Government is direoted to report to General
Headquarters, Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers, the following infor-
mation on each license issued or amended:
(1) Number and title of each patent licensed;
(2) Name of licensor;
(3) Name of licensee;
(4) Basis on which royalty is to be calculated;
(5) Rate of royalty 'to be paid.
b. Initial report containing the above information will be sub-
mitted within sixty (60) days of the date of this memorandum, covering
the period 8 March 1946 through 31 March 1948. Subsequent reports wi11
be rendered quarterly as of the close of business on the last day of each
quarter and will reach General Headquarters, Supreme Commander for the
Allied Powers not later than the last day of the month following the end
of cach quarter.
FOR THE SUPREME COMMANDER:
JF. Bradshew
R. M. LEVY,
for

Gentlemen:
On September 30, 1926, the International Standard
Electric Corporation, a Delaware corporation having an office at
67 Broad Styeet, New York, New York, entered into an agreement
with the NYppon Electric Company, Ltd., a Japanese corporation
having its office at #2 Mita, Shikokumachi, Shibaku, Tokyo, Japan.
This agreement was replaced on January 10, 1938 by a similar
agreement which was not to expire until September 30, 1950. Both
of these agreements related to engineering, manufacturing and sales
services and to an exchange of patent information and. rights.
Under the terms of both of the agreements the International
Standard Electric Corporation granted the Nippon Electric Company
the right to take, hold and maintain in the name of the Nippon
Company certain patents on inventions owmed or controlled by International
Standard Electrid Corporation.
We have been advised that under the first agreement some
patents on such inventions were taken out in the name of or transferred
to the Nippon Electric Company. We believe that many of the patents
obtained under the provisions of the second agreement were also taken
out in or transferred to the name of the Nippon Electric Company.
Although the Nippon Electric Company had the right to take
patents in its name covering inventions owmed or controlled by
CS/V
International Standard Electric Corporation, the agreements provided
that upon their termination whether by lapse of time or otherwise all
the patents and applications for patents held or made by the Nippon
Company for inventions derived from International Standard Electric
Corporation would be assigned to International Standard or its nominees
free and clear of all licenses and incumbrances except that where the
agreement was terminated for certain reasons specified in the agreement
Nippon would retain a non-exclusive, non-assignable license under such
patents and applications. Also, in certain cases specified in the
agreement, licensees would retain non-assignable, non-exclusive licenses
under the patents.
894.542/7-748
AUG 26 1948
FILED
ANTERNATIONAL RESOURCES DIVISION
JUL 16 1948
DEPARTMENT OF STATE

=== Page 326 === (confidence 0.99)
-2
We are calling this matter to your attention so that you
will be advised of our interest in some of the patents which may 
be registered in the name of the Nippon Electric Company. We are
prepared to present formal evidence of our ownership of the patents on
inventions osmed by International Standard Electric Corporation and
desire to obtain a transfer of such patents to the International
Standard Electric Corporation as soon as they can be determined.
We are investigating the matter further and expect to have, in
the near future, a complete list of Japanese patents which were obtained
on inventions owmed and controlled by International Standard and which
although they may now be registered in the name of the Nippon Hlectric
Company are the property of International Standard. As soon as we have
received further information in connection with the patents involved we
shall advise you further as to the specific patents involved.
Very truly yours,

XR

=== Page 329 === (confidence 0.98)
Mr. Saltsman
July 8, 1948
G. W. Lewis
Policy Towards Patents and Utility Models in Japan.
I have been unable, in spite of repeated efforts over the past week,
to got from the Army a statement of whether they do or do not conour in the
instructions on the above subject which are contained in a former SaNACC
paper and in two FEC papers (C1-284/4 and C1-311). This is one of the PMC
matters on which the U.S. representative is currently uninstructed.
At one time the Army had a substantive objection to this paper. They
did not wish the paper to indicate that there would be no secret Japanese
patents withheld when in fact the Army wished to screen certain of these
patents with the view possibly to withhold soms from general publicatien.
However, the Army has now determined that they do not in fact wish to with-
M
hold any of these patents. Col. Cheseldine tells me that there appears to
be no substantive objection to the paper any where in the Army. The difficulty
appears to be that in General Draper's absence no one there wishes to take
the responsibility of approving a paper that has been caught in the SANAcC
"log jam" and in addition of approving instructions on this matter to our
FEC representative. There appears to be also a reluctanoe to move on this
particular paper since it was one of those mentioned in the recent letter
from Mr. Royall in which he complained that we had gone too far in outlining
U.S. positions in FEC which did not have Army approval. It seems te me, and
Col. Cheseldine agrees, that this latter point has no bearing on the questien
at issue now since it has to do with past action. The Army, hovever, mants
an answer to Mr. Royall's letter before they move. Such an answer has been
delayed by discussions within the Department on points which have nothing to
do with this particular question. Mr. Hemmendinger is presently redrafting
the reply to Mr. Boyall in an effort to get it out of the Department before
the end of the week.
I was told this morning that Mr. Royall was personelly interested in
this particular paper and therefore General Noce feels that he cannet give
Army concurrence. He is not, as I understand it, even going to take up the
matter with Secretary Royall. I, therefore, recommend that you either
(a) discuss the matter with General Noce with the view to asking him to got
Army clearance, or (b) ask Mr. Royall directly for Army clearance.
The specific papers involved are Policy Towards Patents and Utility
Models in Japan (C1-284/4) and Recommendations Regarding Japanese-Owned Patents,
Utility Models and Designs in Territories of Members of the United Mations,
Report of Patents Subcommittee (Cl-3ll). As stated above, these papers both
stem from and are consistent with the former SANACC 399.

=== Page 331 === (confidence 0.99)
FEC-RESTRICTED
ENCLOSURE
POLICY TOWARDS PATENTS AND UTILITY MODELS IN JAPAN
The Far Eastern Commission decides as a matter of policy
the following:
1. The Japanese Patent Office
The Japanese Patent Office should continue to exercise
its functions, in such manner as will fulfill the provisions
in paragraphs 3, 4 and 5 below. The Japanese Patent Law should
contain explicit provisions requiring disclosure in the speci-
fications or drawirgs of all information necessary to the
working of a patented invention, and such provisions should be
strictly enforced. The Patent Office should resume the publi-
cation of official gazettes and patent specifications.
2. Patent 1
Rights in Japan
No action should be taken to interfere with patent
rights in Japan which now exist, or which are restored, or
which are newly acquired during the period of occupation, except
a. where the continued protection of such rights
is in conflict with policies established or to be established
in accordance with the terms of reference f the Far Eastern
Commission.
b. where such rights interfere with such pro-
duction, use, sale or importation of products as are necessary
to carry out the purposes of the occupation. In such cases
the Supreme Commarder should request the Japanese Government
to invoke appropriate provisions of the Japanese Patent Law
to the extent that such action may provide a remedy.
3.
Secret Patents in Japan
The future maintenance in secrecy of any patent in
Japan should be rrohibited, and existing secret patents should
be published and treated in the same manner as all other patents.
4. Patents in Japan Owned by Allied Nationals
(For the purposes of this paragraph: An Allied national
is one whose property was treated as enemy property by the
Japanese Government, or whose property rights lapsed owing to
the existence of hostilities between Japan and the country of
that national.
For the purposes of carrying out the provisions of this
paragraph: The "effective date of loss" applicable to the
patents or applications therefor of Allied nationals should be
the date of entry of the country of that national into war with
Japan. When the date of entry into war is clearly inapplicable
to the case of a particular Allied national, as defined above,
the "effective date of loss" should be the actual date of
sequestration of his patent, or the date on which his patent
rights lapsed.)
1
The term "patent" as used herein shall be interpreted to
include those patents designated as "registered utility
models".

=== Page 332 === (confidence 0.99)
FEC-RESTRICTED
a. Patents existing in Japan on the effective date of
loss or granted thereafter on applications filed before that date
which were owned or had been applied for by Allied nationals and
which have since lapsed, or have been invalidated or sequestered
or otherwise taken away from such owners without their free consent
and without reasonable compensation to them, should be subject to
revalidation and restoration upon request therefor made by the
Allied national owners within a reasonable period and in a manner
as may be provided by the Supreme Commander. To such cases the
following provisions should apply:
(1)
The owner to whom such patent is restored should
remit to the Japanese Government any funds paid
to him or credited to his account which are
proceeds from sale of the patent right.
(2)
Payment of any special or regular fees that may
have accrued to the Japanese Government with
respect to such patents from the effective date
of loss to date of such restoration should not
be required.
(3)
Such owner should be afforded a reasonable time
withir. which to perform all legally required
acts in connection with restoration, revalida-
tion, and/or maintenance of his rights which
could not be performed because of the existence
of hostilities or of similar restrictive con-
ditions obtaining from the effective date of loss
to the dete of restoration.
(4)
The owner of a patent so restored or revalidated
should be entitled to all the rights and privi-
leges to which any national of any country is
entitled under the Japanese patent law axisting
at the time of such restoration.
b.
Extension
At the request of the Allied naticnal owner made
within a reasonable time (in such form and prior to such date as
may be prescribed by the Supreme Commander), the period of dura-
tion of any Japanese patent owned by him on the effective date of
loss or granted thereafter on an application filed before that
date should be extended beyond its normal expiration date. Such
extension should be for a period equal to that from the effective
date of loss to a suitable date fixed by the Supreme Commander,
provided that such owner waives all claim to royalties for use of
the patent from the effective date of loss to the date so fixed,
and remits any funds received by him or credited to him in a
blocked account as royalties for such use.
c. Royalties
Tho Supreme Commander should not be responsible for
obtaining, or attempting to obtain, on behalf of Allied nationals
compensation for any use made of their patents prior to date of
restoration. However, the Supreme Commander should make available
to Allied national owners information as to funds credited to
their accounts which constitute royalties for use of their rights
from the effective date of loss to date of restoration. If the
Allied national owner elects to receive such funds in lieu of an
extension of term as provided in para. b above, he should be
entitled to do so on the same basis as other funds are restored
to Allied national property owners in Japan.
- 2 -
C1-284/3

=== Page 333 === (confidence 0.99)
FEC-RESTRICTED
d. Applications Pending an Effective Date of Loss
Applications filed by Allied nationals for patents
in Japan which were pending on the effective date of loss and on
which patents were not issued should, upon request of the Allied
national applicant within a reasonable time and in a manner pre-
scribed by the Supreme Commander, be reinstated as pending applica-
tions in the Japanese Patent Office and be restored to the Ailied
national applicant. Any patents thereafter issued on such
applications should be treated in a manner which conforms generally
with applicable provisions of subparagraph 4 a above.
5. Priority Through the International Convention
Nationals of countries at war with Japan who had duly
filed in any country which is a member of the Union for the
Protection of Industrial Property the first application for a
patent not earlier than twelve months before the effective date
of loss should be entitled, within twelve months after the date,
as determined by the Supreme Commander, on which such governments
and nationals are again permitted to apply for patents directly
to the Japanese Patent Office and to obtain legal services
necessary for this purpose, to apply for corresponding rights in
Japan with a right of priority based upon the previous first
filing of the applicaticn. Third porties who have manufactured,
used, sold or leased the subject matter of any Japanese patent
obtained with this priority should not be liable for infringement
on account of such use but should be enabled to continue such use
after the filing of the applicntion only under the terms of a non-
exclusive license which the patent owner should be required to
grant on terms providing for reasonable royelties as fixed by an
agency to be authorized to make such determinations. Payment of
such royalties should be subject to pplicable financial regula-
tions in effect in J~pan at the time they are paid.

=== Page 335 === (confidence 1.00)
FEC-RESTRICTED
ENCLOSURE
RECOMMENDATIONS REGARDING JAPANESE-OWNED PATENTS, UTILITY MODELS
AND DESIGNS IN TERRITORIES OF MEMBERS OF THE UNITED NATIONS
Report of Patents Subcommittee
The Far Eastern Commission recommends countries which have
been at war with Japan to adopt the following policy in respect
of Japanese-owned patents, utiiity models and designs within
their jurisdiction.
1. All former Japanese-owned patents, in the possession
or control of a country under laws and regulations relating
to Japanese-owned property, which have not ceased or been
dedicated to the public, should be made available to the
public, either by dedication to the public, or by placing
in the public domain or by continuously offering for licen-
sing without royalty, in each case without charges or
restriction, except so far as restriction is necessary to
protect rights which have been lawfully granted to or
acquired by any non-enemy national and except that no rights
need be made available to nationals of countries which do
not, before December 31, 1948, notify the Far Eastern Com-
mission that they intend to adopt the provisions of this
policy.
2. The provisions of international conventions relating
to patents to which Japan was a party on the 6th December
1941, should be applied to Japan, and applications for
protection should be entertained in respect of inventions
made by Japanese after the 1st January 1946, except for
inventions relating to articles, the manufacture, ownership
or operation of which, or to processes, the ownership or
operation of which, is forbidden to the Japanese by poli-
cies established in accordance with the Terms of Reference
of the Far Eastern Commission.
3. Unpublished applications for patents abandoned or
refused during the war should be published or otherwise
made available to the public.
4. All existing rights of nationals of members of the
United Nations in Japanese-owned patents should be protec-
ted.
5. This policy applies also to those patents designated
as "registered utility models" and, to the extent appli-
cable, to "registered designs".

MEMORANDUM FOR: JAPANESE GOVERNLENT
SUBJECT:
Reclassification of Secret Patents and Utility Models
1. Reference is made to memorandum for the Japanese Government,
file AG O72 (1 Jun 48)CFC/FP, SCAPIN 1902, 1 June 1948, subject, "Licensing
of Government-Owned Patents," from General Haadquarters, Supreme Commander
for the Allied Powers.
2. Patents and utility models hitherto classified as secret shall
be removed from such classification and henceforth shall be treated in
like manner as all other patents and utility models in the reoords of
the Patent Office.
3. Pending applications for patents or utility models hitherto
classified as secret shall be removed from such classification and shall
be processed in like manner as all other such applications.
4. Patents and utility models hitherto classified as seoret and
registered in the name of the Japanese Government, its agenoies or sub-
divisions, shall be subject to licensing under the provisions of reference 1
above, provided that they do not relate to weapons or means of war.

randu. of July 29, 1948 to the Japenese Government entitled Trademarks,
Trade Names, and Company Names and Marks used in Common by Companies
in the Same Chain of Capital with Designated Holding Companies and
Designated Excessive Coucentrations."
The cnclosed directive requires the Japanese Government to amend
by Cabinet order Imperial crdinance No. 567 of 1946, to the end that
the Holding Company Liquidation Commission may prohibit any company
from using a trademark or trade name when it has heretofere been used
ITP
in common by a holdin company designated for dissolution and by its
affiliates or subsidiaries, or when it has been used in common by com-
panies designated as excessive ecenomic concentrations scheduled for
FE
reorganization and by other members of the samc combination of capital.
Certain family and company names are specifically listed as being li-

OFD

L
removing from eligibility for registratior. and exclusive use under the
Trademark Law or the Commercial or Civil Codes any mark the use of
which by previous owners has been enjoined by the Holding Company
Liquidation Commission. Pending such legislation, the measure is to
DC/K
be enforced by Cabinet crder.
CS/A
The instruction was prepared, according to officers of the Anti-
Trust and Cartels Division of this Hcadquarters, upon the theory that
the exclusive use of the trademarks of well-known combinations by
SEP 16 1948
their successor companies would serve to perpetuate the benefits of
monopolies which it is the policy of this Headquarters to destroy.
FILED
It was further decided that the best way to prevent such lingering
894.542/8
abuees would not be to ban the marks completely and thus leave them
intact at the end of the Occupation, but rather to debase them by
allowing free and unrestricted use by any and all companies. Thus
the HCLC

Dear Mr. Saltzmans
In two separate meetings on July 26, 1948, representatives of
the National Foreign Trade Council, including the writer, conferred with
you and Mr. William H. Draper, Jr., Under Seoretary of the Army, conoern-
XR
ing problems of American business in Japan. Although protection of
industrial property located in Japan owned by United States nationals is
a matter of primary importance, this subjeot was referred to casually in
the meetings with you and Mr. Draper. However, in the meeting with
894.543
Mr. Draper, it was suggested that this topio be disoussed in detail at a
future meeting with you and your associates.
CS/N
For your information a preliminary meeting was held on
April 21, 1948, when NFTC representatives discussed this matter with the
following members of the Department of State: Messrs. Philander P. Claxton Jr.,
Roswell H. Whitman, Roger Dixon, and R. Barr Smith. Since that time no
information concerning any change in the Japanese patent or trade mark
situation has been reoeived.
Although a prooedure for the return of United States physical
property located in Japan has been instituted, no provision has been made
for the return of industrial property. Early return of Amerioan owmed
patents and trade marks is essential to effective participation by Ameri-
can industry in the effort to revive the Japanese economy. Concurrently,
the Japanese patent system should be restored and undesirable features
modified.
Attached is an outline of proposals, enactment of which it is
894.542/8.3148
believed would result in protection of American rights and reestablishment
of e sound Japanese patent law. Several of these suggestions parallel the
United States government's policy concerning the patent situation in Japan
as reported by the Department of State representatives in the conversation
held on April 21, 1948.

=== Page 351 === (confidence 0.98)
PROPOSALS OF NFTC CONCERNING (1) RIGHTS OF UNITED NATIONALS TO
INDUSTRIAL PROPERTY LOCATED IN JAPAN AND (2) JAPANESE PATENT LAW.
PATENTS
1. The beneficial ownership of patents owned by United States nationals
should be recognized, and all United States patents inoluding those bene-
ficially owmed, should be restored immediately. U. S. patents which,
during the war, (1) were sequestered, (2) canoelled, or (3) lapsed for
non-payment of fees, should be restored to their owmers.
2. The beneficial interest of United States nationals in patent-rights
agreement shall be restored to the beneficial owners.
3. The terms of patents restored to United States nationals should be
extended for a period equal to the period from the out-break of the war
to the time the patent is restored to the United States national.
4. Payment of taxes and fees, liability for which ocourred during the
war, should be waived.
5. United States nationals whose patents have been restored, shall have
the right to recover damages for infringement and to institute injunotive
proceedings to prevent infringement.
6. United States nationals shall have a right of priority based upon the
previous filing of an application in the territory of any member of
the international convention. This right shall include applications for
registrations filed within a period of 12 months before the commenoe-
ment of the war between the United States and Japan.
7. The Japanese petent office should be re-established and applications
accepted from both foreigners and Japenese.
8. Reinstatement of Japanese patent law without fundamental change,
which according to Department of State representatives, is present United
States government's policy, should be acoeptable to United States business
if modified in several respects as noted below, and if properly enforoed.
However, if the present policy is revised and fundamental changes are
contemplated, it is recommended that changes in the law be based on the
United States patent law which has been found to be most suitable to an
industrial economy. In any event, if complete change is not made, it is
recommended that the Japenese law be modified in the following respeots:
a. The unusually severe penalties should be made less stringent.
b. Provisions for opposition proceeding, tending to enoourage
litigation, should be eliminated.
C.
The requirement for payment of annual taxes, whioh frequently
imposes hardships on investors of limited resouroes, should be
eliminated.
27×3

=== Page 352 === (confidence 0.98)
-2-
do
The provisions for working patents should be eliminated from
the law. In addition to the general trend away from suoh
requirements, the practical reason for their deletion may
be found in the time lag between invention and produotion
on any given produot.
TRADE MARKS
1. The beneficial ownership of trade marks should be reoognized and all
trade marks owned by United States nationals should be restored immediately.
2. The right to sue for infringement and the right to injunctive pro-
oeeding to prevent the mis-use of trade marks should be provided.
MARK OF ORIGIN
1. In order to eliminate unethical competition with certain American pro-
duots by Japanese exporters, it is recommended that all artioles exported
from Japan clearly indicate the country of origin. This is partioulerly
important in cases of Japan copying artioles manufactured by United States
firms who hold themselves ready to supply repair parts on patented artioles.
